I guess… I’m here because I’ve started doing things that kind of scare me. Like at work, I’ll just walk out in the middle of a deployment, which is not something I do—I’m a systems analyst, so that’s basically the worst possible time to disappear.

I don’t know if I’d call it watching myself from outside my body. That sounds… more dramatic than what I notice.

It’s more like I’m already halfway to the parking lot before I’ve fully decided to leave, if that makes sense. I don’t think so. Not like… a voice or some force steering me, if that’s what you mean.

It’s more like I get on a track and then realize I’ve been following it without checking why. I used to be very controlled about stress, maybe too controlled—my old manager used to joke that I made spreadsheets for my spreadsheets—but this doesn’t feel like me. That’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: When you’re walking out during work or driving around late, do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or like things around you seem unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it watching myself from outside my body. That sounds… more dramatic than what I notice.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already halfway to the parking lot before I’ve fully decided to leave, if that makes sense. Like when I’m deep in a system issue and someone starts talking about one little error message, but the actual problem is somewhere else entirely—sorry, that’s a work analogy. I do that a lot.\n\nThings don’t exactly look unreal, I don’t think. I just get kind of disconnected from the plan I was supposed to be following. Then later I’m like, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts or actions were not entirely your own, or like something else is guiding you to do these things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… a voice or some force steering me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like I get on a track and then realize I’ve been following it without checking why. Which sounds silly, because I spend all day checking why things happen—logs, permissions, tiny configuration changes that no one admits they made. But with me, it’s just… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it feels like something else is guiding me. I just don’t always feel like I’ve thought it through before I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not fully in control of yourself, or do you feel compelled to act even though you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “not fully in control” is closer than “compelled,” maybe. I’m not sitting there thinking, this makes sense, I should do this. It’s more like I’m already doing it before the sensible part of me catches up.\n\nLike at work, leaving during a deployment—that’s not just inconvenient, that’s the kind of thing I would normally be the person preventing someone else from doing. And the driving around late with no real plan… I don’t know. I don’t like that. It scares me afterward because it’s not how I’ve handled stress before.\n\nDoctor: Before you find yourself walking out during work or driving around late, do you ever feel a strong urge or inner tension that makes it hard to resist doing those things?\n\nPatient: I think there is tension before it, yeah, but I don’t always recognize it as an “urge” until after. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding, “I’m going to go do something reckless now.” It’s more like everything gets too tight inside and then suddenly I’m moving.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. Sort of… I don’t know if I’d call it fully unaware. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not steering very well.

I don’t think so… not like I’m floating above myself or actually watching from outside my body.

I’ve started doing things that scare me—walking out in the middle of deployments, driving around late at night with no real plan. And I’m not someone who does that. I mean, normally I’m the person with the checklist, the rollback plan, the backup person on call… sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, there’s this build-up, and then it feels hard to stop myself before I’m already doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you walk out during a deployment or drive around late at night, do you feel a sense of relief, pleasure, or any kind of emotional release, or do you feel anxious and upset with yourself right away?\n\nPatient: Not pleasure, no. I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan. And that’s just… not how I’ve ever handled stress before. That’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: When you’re walking out during work or driving around late, do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or like things around you seem unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it watching myself from outside my body. That sounds… more dramatic than what I notice.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already halfway to the parking lot before I’ve fully decided to leave, if that makes sense. Like when I’m deep in a system issue and someone starts talking about one little error message, but the actual problem is somewhere else entirely—sorry, that’s a work analogy. I do that a lot.\n\nThings don’t exactly look unreal, I don’t think. I just get kind of disconnected from the plan I was supposed to be following. Then later I’m like, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts or actions were not entirely your own, or like something else is guiding you to do these things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… a voice or some force steering me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like I get on a track and then realize I’ve been following it without checking why. Which sounds silly, because I spend all day checking why things happen—logs, permissions, tiny configuration changes that no one admits they made. But with me, it’s just… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it feels like something else is guiding me. I just don’t always feel like I’ve thought it through before I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not fully in control of yourself, or do you feel compelled to act even though you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “not fully in control” is closer than “compelled,” maybe. I’m not sitting there thinking, this makes sense, I should do this. It’s more like I’m already doing it before the sensible part of me catches up.\n\nLike at work, leaving during a deployment—that’s not just inconvenient, that’s the kind of thing I would normally be the person preventing someone else from doing. And the driving around late with no real plan… I don’t know. I don’t like that. It scares me afterward because it’s not how I’ve handled stress before.\n\nDoctor: Before you find yourself walking out during work or driving around late, do you ever feel a strong urge or inner tension that makes it hard to resist doing those things?\n\nPatient: I think there is tension before it, yeah, but I don’t always recognize it as an “urge” until after. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding, “I’m going to go do something reckless now.” It’s more like everything gets too tight inside and then suddenly I’m moving.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me—walking out in the middle of deployments, driving around late at night with no real plan. And I’m not someone who does that. I mean, normally I’m the person with the checklist, the rollback plan, the backup person on call… sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, there’s this build-up, and then it feels hard to stop myself before I’m already doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you walk out during a deployment or drive around late at night, do you feel a sense of relief, pleasure, or any kind of emotional release, or do you feel anxious and upset with yourself right away?\n\nPatient: Not pleasure, no. I don’t think relief either, not really. Maybe there’s a second where the pressure drops because I’m out of the situation, but then pretty quickly I’m thinking, what are you doing?\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan. And that’s just… not how I’ve ever handled stress before. I’m usually the one making sure nothing breaks, and then suddenly I’m the thing that’s breaking the plan, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're on autopilot or later realize you weren't fully aware of what you were doing while it was happening?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I don’t know if I’d call it fully unaware. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not steering very well.\n\nLike, I’ll realize I’m already walking out, or I’m in the car driving around late with no real plan, and then my brain catches up and goes, what are you doing? That’s part of why I came in, honestly. I’ve started doing things that scare me, and that’s just not how I usually handle things.\n\nI’m usually very controlled at work—plans, checklists, all of that—and then suddenly I’m the person leaving in the middle of a deployment. It feels… not like me.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don't know who you are or have trouble remembering what's happened afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that exactly. I mean, I get shaken afterward, and I’ll be trying to piece together why I did it, but it’s not like I don’t know who I am.\n\nRemembering… I don’t know, I remember the basics. I might miss little details because my head is spinning, kind of like when you leave a meeting and realize you didn’t absorb half of what someone said because you were thinking about the server logs or the schedule slipping—sorry, that’s probably not the same thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed waking up later or having big blank spots, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a fog or lose track of time, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Fog” might be the right word sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose time exactly.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets kind of narrowed down, if that makes sense. Like the room feels too bright, or sounds are too much, and then I’ll start thinking about whether I remembered to send some status update at work—which is ridiculous, because that’s not the moment to be worrying about email—but my brain does that.\n\nThe world doesn’t usually look unreal, exactly. More like I’m overwhelmed and trying to get through it. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “That’s not my body,” or “This isn’t real,” if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're not really yourself or like you're a different person, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. And the driving around late with no real plan… I don’t know. I don’t like that. It scares me afterward because it’s not how I’ve handled stress before.\n\nDoctor: Before you find yourself walking out during work or driving around late, do you ever feel a strong urge or inner tension that makes it hard to resist doing those things?\n\nPatient: I think there is tension before it, yeah, but I don’t always recognize it as an “urge” until after. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding, “I’m going to go do something reckless now.” It’s more like everything gets too tight inside and then suddenly I’m moving.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me—walking out in the middle of deployments, driving around late at night with no real plan. And I’m not someone who does that. I mean, normally I’m the person with the checklist, the rollback plan, the backup person on call… sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, there’s this build-up, and then it feels hard to stop myself before I’m already doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you walk out during a deployment or drive around late at night, do you feel a sense of relief, pleasure, or any kind of emotional release, or do you feel anxious and upset with yourself right away?\n\nPatient: Not pleasure, no. I don’t think relief either, not really. Maybe there’s a second where the pressure drops because I’m out of the situation, but then pretty quickly I’m thinking, what are you doing?\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan. And that’s just… not how I’ve ever handled stress before. I’m usually the one making sure nothing breaks, and then suddenly I’m the thing that’s breaking the plan, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're on autopilot or later realize you weren't fully aware of what you were doing while it was happening?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I don’t know if I’d call it fully unaware. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not steering very well.\n\nLike, I’ll realize I’m already walking out, or I’m in the car driving around late with no real plan, and then my brain catches up and goes, what are you doing? That’s part of why I came in, honestly. I’ve started doing things that scare me, and that’s just not how I usually handle things.\n\nI’m usually very controlled at work—plans, checklists, all of that—and then suddenly I’m the person leaving in the middle of a deployment. It feels… not like me.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don't know who you are or have trouble remembering what's happened afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that exactly. Like when I’m troubleshooting something and I realize I’ve been staring at the same screen for twenty minutes, except this feels worse and less useful. Sorry, that’s probably not a great comparison.\n\nI haven’t noticed big chunks of time missing. Maybe a few minutes feel blurry afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s losing track of time or just being worked up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your body, like you were watching yourself from the outside, or felt like the world around you seemed strange or unreal, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m floating above myself or actually watching from outside my body.\n\nSometimes things feel kind of off when I’m really worked up, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like the room feels too bright, or sounds are too much, and then I’ll start thinking about whether I remembered to send some status update at work—which is ridiculous, because that’s not the moment to be worrying about email—but my brain does that.\n\nThe world doesn’t usually look unreal, exactly. More like I’m overwhelmed and trying to get through it. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “That’s not my body,” or “This isn’t real,” if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're not really yourself or like you're a different person, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I haven’t felt like I’m a different person. More like I’m still me, just… not handling things very well in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were losing your sense of who you are or felt detached from yourself, even briefly, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I lose who I am, exactly.\n\nI can get… kind of caught in it, and then afterward I’ll think, that didn’t feel like me at my best, but not like I’m detached from myself. More like I’m still in there, just everything is too much at once.\n\nSorry, I’m probably splitting hairs. I haven’t really noticed feeling separated from myself, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don’t recognize yourself or like you’re not the same person you usually are, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. I recognize myself. I might feel embarrassed afterward, like I didn’t handle it the way I normally would, but I don’t look in the mirror or think, who is that, or anything like that.\n\nI guess sometimes when I’m stressed I get very… task-focused, like I’ll suddenly be thinking about some tiny thing I forgot to do, and then later it seems strange that my mind went there. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from across the room or anything.

The “world not real” part is a little harder to answer, because sometimes when I’m overwhelmed everything can feel kind of distant, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean or if it’s just me zoning out. Like when I’m staring at a screen too long and the numbers all blur together, and then someone asks me something and I realize I’ve been sitting there holding my breath. But that’s not really the world feeling fake, I don’t think.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that. Not in the way you’re describing.

No, I don’t think I’ve ever felt completely disconnected like that. Not like I’m a different person or watching myself from inside my own body.

I mean, I can get really overwhelmed and kind of scattered, and then I’ll start thinking about some random thing—like whether I answered an email or if I left a file open at work—and that can make me seem absent, I guess. My sister says I do that sometimes, drift off mid-conversation, but I think that’s just my brain jumping tracks.

But the way you’re describing it… being an observer in my own body… no, I don’t think so.

I wouldn’t say it feels like something else is guiding me. I just don’t always feel like I’ve thought it through before I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not fully in control of yourself, or do you feel compelled to act even though you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “not fully in control” is closer than “compelled,” maybe. I’m not sitting there thinking, this makes sense, I should do this. It’s more like I’m already doing it before the sensible part of me catches up.\n\nLike at work, leaving during a deployment—that’s not just inconvenient, that’s the kind of thing I would normally be the person preventing someone else from doing. And the driving around late with no real plan… I don’t know. I don’t like that. It scares me afterward because it’s not how I’ve handled stress before.\n\nDoctor: Before you find yourself walking out during work or driving around late, do you ever feel a strong urge or inner tension that makes it hard to resist doing those things?\n\nPatient: I think there is tension before it, yeah, but I don’t always recognize it as an “urge” until after. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding, “I’m going to go do something reckless now.” It’s more like everything gets too tight inside and then suddenly I’m moving.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me—walking out in the middle of deployments, driving around late at night with no real plan. And I’m not someone who does that. I don’t think relief either, not really. Maybe there’s a second where the pressure drops because I’m out of the situation, but then pretty quickly I’m thinking, what are you doing?\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan. And that’s just… not how I’ve ever handled stress before. I’m usually the one making sure nothing breaks, and then suddenly I’m the thing that’s breaking the plan, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're on autopilot or later realize you weren't fully aware of what you were doing while it was happening?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I don’t know if I’d call it fully unaware. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not steering very well.\n\nLike, I’ll realize I’m already walking out, or I’m in the car driving around late with no real plan, and then my brain catches up and goes, what are you doing? That’s part of why I came in, honestly. I’ve started doing things that scare me, and that’s just not how I usually handle things.\n\nI’m usually very controlled at work—plans, checklists, all of that—and then suddenly I’m the person leaving in the middle of a deployment. It feels… not like me.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don't know who you are or have trouble remembering what's happened afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that exactly. I mean, I get shaken afterward, and I’ll be trying to piece together why I did it, but it’s not like I don’t know who I am.\n\nRemembering… I don’t know, I remember the basics. I might miss little details because my head is spinning, kind of like when you leave a meeting and realize you didn’t absorb half of what someone said because you were thinking about the server logs or the schedule slipping—sorry, that’s probably not the same thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed waking up later or having big blank spots, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a fog or lose track of time, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Fog” might be the right word sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose time exactly.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets kind of narrowed down, if that makes sense. Like when I’m troubleshooting something and I realize I’ve been staring at the same screen for twenty minutes, except this feels worse and less useful. Sorry, that’s probably not a great comparison.\n\nI haven’t noticed big chunks of time missing. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “That’s not my body,” or “This isn’t real,” if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're not really yourself or like you're a different person, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but I’ve definitely stopped doing the things I used to care about.

Yeah… even when nothing is actively happening, I still don’t really enjoy those things the way I used to.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F32.9, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve started doing things that kind of scare me. Like at work, I’ll just walk out in the middle of a deployment, which is not something I do—I’m a systems analyst, so that’s basically the worst possible time to disappear.\n\nAnd then other nights I’ll just drive around late with no plan, like I don’t even know where I’m going. I used to be very controlled about stress, maybe too controlled—my old manager used to joke that I made spreadsheets for my spreadsheets—but this doesn’t feel like me. That’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: When you’re walking out during work or driving around late, do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or like things around you seem unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it watching myself from outside my body. That sounds… more dramatic than what I notice.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already halfway to the parking lot before I’ve fully decided to leave, if that makes sense. Like when I’m deep in a system issue and someone starts talking about one little error message, but the actual problem is somewhere else entirely—sorry, that’s a work analogy. I do that a lot.\n\nThings don’t exactly look unreal, I don’t think. I just get kind of disconnected from the plan I was supposed to be following. Then later I’m like, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts or actions were not entirely your own, or like something else is guiding you to do these things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… a voice or some force steering me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like I get on a track and then realize I’ve been following it without checking why. Which sounds silly, because I spend all day checking why things happen—logs, permissions, tiny configuration changes that no one admits they made. But with me, it’s just… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it feels like something else is guiding me. I just don’t always feel like I’ve thought it through before I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not fully in control of yourself, or do you feel compelled to act even though you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “not fully in control” is closer than “compelled,” maybe. I’m not sitting there thinking, this makes sense, I should do this. It’s more like I’m already doing it before the sensible part of me catches up.\n\nLike at work, leaving during a deployment—that’s not just inconvenient, that’s the kind of thing I would normally be the person preventing someone else from doing. And the driving around late with no real plan… I don’t know. I don’t like that. It scares me afterward because it’s not how I’ve handled stress before.\n\nDoctor: Before you find yourself walking out during work or driving around late, do you ever feel a strong urge or inner tension that makes it hard to resist doing those things?\n\nPatient: I think there is tension before it, yeah, but I don’t always recognize it as an “urge” until after. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding, “I’m going to go do something reckless now.” It’s more like everything gets too tight inside and then suddenly I’m moving.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me—walking out in the middle of deployments, driving around late at night with no real plan. And I’m not someone who does that. I mean, normally I’m the person with the checklist, the rollback plan, the backup person on call… sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, there’s this build-up, and then it feels hard to stop myself before I’m already doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you walk out during a deployment or drive around late at night, do you feel a sense of relief, pleasure, or any kind of emotional release, or do you feel anxious and upset with yourself right away?\n\nPatient: Not pleasure, no. I don’t think relief either, not really. Maybe there’s a second where the pressure drops because I’m out of the situation, but then pretty quickly I’m thinking, what are you doing?\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan. And that’s just… not how I’ve ever handled stress before. I’m usually the one making sure nothing breaks, and then suddenly I’m the thing that’s breaking the plan, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're on autopilot or later realize you weren't fully aware of what you were doing while it was happening?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I don’t know if I’d call it fully unaware. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not steering very well.\n\nLike, I’ll realize I’m already walking out, or I’m in the car driving around late with no real plan, and then my brain catches up and goes, what are you doing? That’s part of why I came in, honestly. I’ve started doing things that scare me, and that’s just not how I usually handle things.\n\nI’m usually very controlled at work—plans, checklists, all of that—and then suddenly I’m the person leaving in the middle of a deployment. It feels… not like me.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don't know who you are or have trouble remembering what's happened afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that exactly. I mean, I get shaken afterward, and I’ll be trying to piece together why I did it, but it’s not like I don’t know who I am.\n\nRemembering… I don’t know, I remember the basics. I might miss little details because my head is spinning, kind of like when you leave a meeting and realize you didn’t absorb half of what someone said because you were thinking about the server logs or the schedule slipping—sorry, that’s probably not the same thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed waking up later or having big blank spots, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a fog or lose track of time, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Fog” might be the right word sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose time exactly.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets kind of narrowed down, if that makes sense. Like when I’m troubleshooting something and I realize I’ve been staring at the same screen for twenty minutes, except this feels worse and less useful. Sorry, that’s probably not a great comparison.\n\nI haven’t noticed big chunks of time missing. Maybe a few minutes feel blurry afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s losing track of time or just being worked up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your body, like you were watching yourself from the outside, or felt like the world around you seemed strange or unreal, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m floating above myself or actually watching from outside my body.\n\nSometimes things feel kind of off when I’m really worked up, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like the room feels too bright, or sounds are too much, and then I’ll start thinking about whether I remembered to send some status update at work—which is ridiculous, because that’s not the moment to be worrying about email—but my brain does that.\n\nThe world doesn’t usually look unreal, exactly. More like I’m overwhelmed and trying to get through it. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “That’s not my body,” or “This isn’t real,” if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're not really yourself or like you're a different person, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like I become someone else.\n\nI mean, I might not act like my usual self, I guess—like I’ll look back and think, why did I get that tense or why couldn’t I just answer normally. But that happens at work too sometimes, like if there’s an outage and everyone suddenly expects you to know exactly which system failed first, and you’re trying to sound calm while your brain is doing ten different things. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I haven’t felt like I’m a different person. More like I’m still me, just… not handling things very well in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were losing your sense of who you are or felt detached from yourself, even briefly, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I lose who I am, exactly.\n\nI can get… kind of caught in it, and then afterward I’ll think, that didn’t feel like me at my best, but not like I’m detached from myself. More like I’m still in there, just everything is too much at once.\n\nSorry, I’m probably splitting hairs. I haven’t really noticed feeling separated from myself, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don’t recognize yourself or like you’re not the same person you usually are, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. I recognize myself. I might feel embarrassed afterward, like I didn’t handle it the way I normally would, but I don’t look in the mirror or think, who is that, or anything like that.\n\nI guess sometimes when I’m stressed I get very… task-focused, like I’ll suddenly be thinking about some tiny thing I forgot to do, and then later it seems strange that my mind went there. But it still feels like me. Just me under too much pressure, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were looking at yourself from the outside, or like the world around you wasn’t quite real, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThe “world not real” part is a little harder to answer, because sometimes when I’m overwhelmed everything can feel kind of distant, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean or if it’s just me zoning out. Like when I’m staring at a screen too long and the numbers all blur together, and then someone asks me something and I realize I’ve been sitting there holding my breath. But that’s not really the world feeling fake, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. Not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely disconnected from yourself, as if you were a different person or just an observer in your own body, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever felt completely disconnected like that. Not like I’m a different person or watching myself from inside my own body.\n\nI mean, I can get really overwhelmed and kind of scattered, and then I’ll start thinking about some random thing—like whether I answered an email or if I left a file open at work—and that can make me seem absent, I guess. My sister says I do that sometimes, drift off mid-conversation, but I think that’s just my brain jumping tracks.\n\nBut the way you’re describing it… being an observer in my own body… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things that usually matter to you, especially around the time these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but I’ve definitely stopped doing the things I used to care about.\n\nI used to kind of build my whole week around stuff—salsa class, book club, yoga, having people over and making Guatemalan food. I used to make too much, honestly, like enough for a small village, and then send everyone home with containers. But lately I’ll say I’m going and then cancel, or just not answer the texts, and I end up at home scrolling on my phone or with some show on that I’m not really watching.\n\nIt’s worse around those episodes, I think. Afterward I just don’t want to deal with anyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like salsa class or cooking, even when you’re not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when nothing is actively happening, I still don’t really enjoy those things the way I used to.\n\nI used to plan my whole week around salsa class, book club, yoga, or having people over and cooking Guatemalan food. I’d get excited about it, make lists, buy way too much cilantro—my mother would always say I cook like people are coming from three towns over.\n\nNow I mostly cancel. Or I tell myself I’ll go and then I just… don’t. I sit at home scrolling on my phone or with a show on, but I’m not really watching it. It’s like I know those things used to feel good, but I can’t get myself to care the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah… I mean, even on days when nothing is really happening, when it should be a normal quiet day, I still feel this heaviness.

It’s not always like crying or anything obvious. It’s more like this mix of fear and shame just sitting in my chest. Like I should have handled everything better, or I should’ve known what to do, and then at the same time I’m kind of waiting for something to go wrong again if I relax too much.

That’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: When you’re walking out during work or driving around late, do you feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body or like things around you seem unreal?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it watching myself from outside my body. That sounds… more dramatic than what I notice.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already halfway to the parking lot before I’ve fully decided to leave, if that makes sense. Like when I’m deep in a system issue and someone starts talking about one little error message, but the actual problem is somewhere else entirely—sorry, that’s a work analogy. I do that a lot.\n\nThings don’t exactly look unreal, I don’t think. I just get kind of disconnected from the plan I was supposed to be following. Then later I’m like, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts or actions were not entirely your own, or like something else is guiding you to do these things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… a voice or some force steering me, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like I get on a track and then realize I’ve been following it without checking why. Which sounds silly, because I spend all day checking why things happen—logs, permissions, tiny configuration changes that no one admits they made. But with me, it’s just… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it feels like something else is guiding me. I just don’t always feel like I’ve thought it through before I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not fully in control of yourself, or do you feel compelled to act even though you know it might not make sense?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “not fully in control” is closer than “compelled,” maybe. I’m not sitting there thinking, this makes sense, I should do this. It’s more like I’m already doing it before the sensible part of me catches up.\n\nLike at work, leaving during a deployment—that’s not just inconvenient, that’s the kind of thing I would normally be the person preventing someone else from doing. And the driving around late with no real plan… I don’t know. I don’t like that. It scares me afterward because it’s not how I’ve handled stress before.\n\nDoctor: Before you find yourself walking out during work or driving around late, do you ever feel a strong urge or inner tension that makes it hard to resist doing those things?\n\nPatient: I think there is tension before it, yeah, but I don’t always recognize it as an “urge” until after. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding, “I’m going to go do something reckless now.” It’s more like everything gets too tight inside and then suddenly I’m moving.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me—walking out in the middle of deployments, driving around late at night with no real plan. And I’m not someone who does that. I mean, normally I’m the person with the checklist, the rollback plan, the backup person on call… sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, there’s this build-up, and then it feels hard to stop myself before I’m already doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you walk out during a deployment or drive around late at night, do you feel a sense of relief, pleasure, or any kind of emotional release, or do you feel anxious and upset with yourself right away?\n\nPatient: Not pleasure, no. I don’t think relief either, not really. Maybe there’s a second where the pressure drops because I’m out of the situation, but then pretty quickly I’m thinking, what are you doing?\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan. And that’s just… not how I’ve ever handled stress before. I’m usually the one making sure nothing breaks, and then suddenly I’m the thing that’s breaking the plan, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're on autopilot or later realize you weren't fully aware of what you were doing while it was happening?\n\nPatient: Sort of… I don’t know if I’d call it fully unaware. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not steering very well.\n\nLike, I’ll realize I’m already walking out, or I’m in the car driving around late with no real plan, and then my brain catches up and goes, what are you doing? That’s part of why I came in, honestly. I’ve started doing things that scare me, and that’s just not how I usually handle things.\n\nI’m usually very controlled at work—plans, checklists, all of that—and then suddenly I’m the person leaving in the middle of a deployment. It feels… not like me.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don't know who you are or have trouble remembering what's happened afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that exactly. I mean, I get shaken afterward, and I’ll be trying to piece together why I did it, but it’s not like I don’t know who I am.\n\nRemembering… I don’t know, I remember the basics. I might miss little details because my head is spinning, kind of like when you leave a meeting and realize you didn’t absorb half of what someone said because you were thinking about the server logs or the schedule slipping—sorry, that’s probably not the same thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t noticed waking up later or having big blank spots, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're in a fog or lose track of time, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Fog” might be the right word sometimes, but I don’t know that I lose time exactly.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets kind of narrowed down, if that makes sense. Like when I’m troubleshooting something and I realize I’ve been staring at the same screen for twenty minutes, except this feels worse and less useful. Sorry, that’s probably not a great comparison.\n\nI haven’t noticed big chunks of time missing. Maybe a few minutes feel blurry afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s losing track of time or just being worked up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from your body, like you were watching yourself from the outside, or felt like the world around you seemed strange or unreal, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m floating above myself or actually watching from outside my body.\n\nSometimes things feel kind of off when I’m really worked up, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like the room feels too bright, or sounds are too much, and then I’ll start thinking about whether I remembered to send some status update at work—which is ridiculous, because that’s not the moment to be worrying about email—but my brain does that.\n\nThe world doesn’t usually look unreal, exactly. More like I’m overwhelmed and trying to get through it. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “That’s not my body,” or “This isn’t real,” if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you're not really yourself or like you're a different person, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like I become someone else.\n\nI mean, I might not act like my usual self, I guess—like I’ll look back and think, why did I get that tense or why couldn’t I just answer normally. But that happens at work too sometimes, like if there’s an outage and everyone suddenly expects you to know exactly which system failed first, and you’re trying to sound calm while your brain is doing ten different things. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I haven’t felt like I’m a different person. More like I’m still me, just… not handling things very well in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were losing your sense of who you are or felt detached from yourself, even briefly, during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I lose who I am, exactly.\n\nI can get… kind of caught in it, and then afterward I’ll think, that didn’t feel like me at my best, but not like I’m detached from myself. More like I’m still in there, just everything is too much at once.\n\nSorry, I’m probably splitting hairs. I haven’t really noticed feeling separated from myself, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel like you don’t recognize yourself or like you’re not the same person you usually are, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that, no. I recognize myself. I might feel embarrassed afterward, like I didn’t handle it the way I normally would, but I don’t look in the mirror or think, who is that, or anything like that.\n\nI guess sometimes when I’m stressed I get very… task-focused, like I’ll suddenly be thinking about some tiny thing I forgot to do, and then later it seems strange that my mind went there. But it still feels like me. Just me under too much pressure, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were looking at yourself from the outside, or like the world around you wasn’t quite real, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from across the room or anything.\n\nThe “world not real” part is a little harder to answer, because sometimes when I’m overwhelmed everything can feel kind of distant, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean or if it’s just me zoning out. Like when I’m staring at a screen too long and the numbers all blur together, and then someone asks me something and I realize I’ve been sitting there holding my breath. But that’s not really the world feeling fake, I don’t think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that. Not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely disconnected from yourself, as if you were a different person or just an observer in your own body, even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever felt completely disconnected like that. Not like I’m a different person or watching myself from inside my own body.\n\nI mean, I can get really overwhelmed and kind of scattered, and then I’ll start thinking about some random thing—like whether I answered an email or if I left a file open at work—and that can make me seem absent, I guess. My sister says I do that sometimes, drift off mid-conversation, but I think that’s just my brain jumping tracks.\n\nBut the way you’re describing it… being an observer in my own body… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things that usually matter to you, especially around the time these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but I’ve definitely stopped doing the things I used to care about.\n\nI used to kind of build my whole week around stuff—salsa class, book club, yoga, having people over and making Guatemalan food. I used to make too much, honestly, like enough for a small village, and then send everyone home with containers. But lately I’ll say I’m going and then cancel, or just not answer the texts, and I end up at home scrolling on my phone or with some show on that I’m not really watching.\n\nIt’s worse around those episodes, I think. Afterward I just don’t want to deal with anyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like salsa class or cooking, even when you’re not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when nothing is actively happening, I still don’t really enjoy those things the way I used to.\n\nI used to plan my whole week around salsa class, book club, yoga, or having people over and cooking Guatemalan food. I’d get excited about it, make lists, buy way too much cilantro—my mother would always say I cook like people are coming from three towns over.\n\nNow I mostly cancel. Or I tell myself I’ll go and then I just… don’t. I sit at home scrolling on my phone or with a show on, but I’m not really watching it. It’s like I know those things used to feel good, but I can’t get myself to care the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or having a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, even when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even on days when nothing is really happening, when it should be a normal quiet day, I still feel this heaviness.\n\nIt’s not always like crying or anything obvious. It’s more like this mix of fear and shame just sitting in my chest. Like I should have handled everything better, or I should’ve known what to do, and then at the same time I’m kind of waiting for something to go wrong again if I relax too much.\n\nIt’s funny, I’ll be doing something totally ordinary, like checking a spreadsheet or washing a coffee mug, and suddenly I realize I’m holding my breath. So… yes, I guess I do feel low a lot of the time. It’s just tangled up with feeling on edge and embarrassed with myself."
